###### **ChatGPT said:**

Here\'s a **Python simulation template** to explore **recursive prime
sheaf perturbations** on the mirror map and topological string
amplitudes over local P2\\mathbb{P}\^2P2. This code allows you to evolve
moduli z(t)z(t)z(t), compute perturbed mirror maps, and analyze
deviations in string free energies.

### **🧮 Python Template: Prime-Recursive Mirror and String Simulation**

> python
>
> CopyEdit

-   import numpy as np

-   import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

-   from scipy.special import factorial

-   

-   \# Constants

-   epsilon = 0.01

-   alpha = 1.5

-   prime\_list = \[2, 3, 5, 7\] \# Small primes for perturbation

-   timesteps = 20

-   z0 = 0.01 \# Initial moduli value

-   

-   \# Recursive prime-zeta perturbation

-   def evolve\_z(z\_prev, epsilon, alpha):

-   for p in prime\_list:

-   z\_prev \*= np.exp(-epsilon / p\*\*alpha)

-   return z\_prev

-   

-   \# Mirror map (genus-0): approximate with series

-   def mirror\_map(z, terms=20):

-   t = np.log(z)

-   for n in range(1, terms):

-   coeff = factorial(3\*n) / (factorial(n)\*\*3)

-   t += coeff \* z\*\*n

-   return t

-   

-   \# Topological string genus-0 amplitude (truncated)

-   def F0(z, Nd, dvals):

-   return sum(\[Nd\[i\] / dvals\[i\]\*\*3 \*
    > np.exp(-dvals\[i\]\*np.log(z)) for i in range(len(Nd))\])

-   

-   \# Simulation data containers

-   z\_values = \[z0\]

-   t\_values = \[mirror\_map(z0)\]

-   Nd = \[3, 27, 192, 1350, 9801\] \# Sample Gromov-Witten invariants

-   dvals = np.arange(1, len(Nd)+1)

-   F0\_values = \[F0(z0, Nd, dvals)\]

-   

-   \# Time evolution loop

-   for t in range(1, timesteps):

-   z\_new = evolve\_z(z\_values\[-1\], epsilon, alpha)

-   z\_values.append(z\_new)

-   t\_values.append(mirror\_map(z\_new))

-   F0\_values.append(F0(z\_new, Nd, dvals))

-   

-   \# Plotting

-   plt.figure(figsize=(12, 6))

-   plt.subplot(1, 2, 1)

-   plt.plot(range(timesteps), z\_values, label=\'z(t)\')

-   plt.plot(range(timesteps), t\_values, label=\'mirror map t(z(t))\')

-   plt.xlabel(\'Time\')

-   plt.ylabel(\'Value\')

-   plt.title(\'Recursive Evolution of Moduli and Mirror Map\')

-   plt.legend()

-   

-   plt.subplot(1, 2, 2)

-   plt.plot(range(timesteps), F0\_values, color=\'purple\',
    > label=\'F₀(t)\')

-   plt.xlabel(\'Time\')

-   plt.ylabel(\'Genus-0 Free Energy\')

-   plt.title(\'Prime-Modulated Genus-0 String Amplitude\')

-   plt.legend()

-   plt.tight\_layout()

-   plt.show()

import numpy as np

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from ipywidgets import interact, FloatSlider

\# Define the resonance operator (simplified for illustration)

def T\_C\_next(t, omega, alpha, lambda\_val, DR=3, T\_prev=0, Xi=1):

term1 = np.exp(1j \* omega \* t)

term2 = np.exp(-alpha \* (9 - DR))

term3 = np.special.j0(2 \* np.pi \* DR / 9)

laplacian = lambda\_val \* 0.1 \# Placeholder for Laplacian term

return term1 \* term2 \* term3 \* Xi \* (1 + laplacian)

\# Interactive plotting function

def plot\_resonance(omega=1.0, alpha=0.1, lambda\_val=0.1):

t\_vals = np.linspace(0, 10, 100)

T\_vals = \[T\_C\_next(t, omega, alpha, lambda\_val) for t in t\_vals\]

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))

plt.plot(t\_vals, np.abs(T\_vals), label=\"\|T\_C(t)\|\")

plt.plot(t\_vals, np.angle(T\_vals), label=\"arg(T\_C(t))\")

plt.xlabel(\"Time t\")

plt.ylabel(\"Resonance Value\")

plt.legend()

plt.grid()

plt.show()

\# Create interactive sliders

interact(plot\_resonance,

omega=FloatSlider(value=1.0, min=0.1, max=5.0, step=0.1,
description=\'ω\'),

alpha=FloatSlider(value=0.1, min=0.01, max=1.0, step=0.01,
description=\'α(t)\'),

lambda\_val=FloatSlider(value=0.1, min=0.01, max=1.0, step=0.01,
description=\'λ\'))

### **🔁 Expanded Definition and Purpose**

The **Goldbach-Harmonic Operator** is a boolean function designed to
return 1 when an integer CC meets a precise conjunction of constraints:

1.  CC must be an **odd integer** greater than 5.

    > Pythagorean numerology and Nikola Tesla\'s 3--6--9 triangle.

from tensorflow.keras import layers, models

def build\_alpha\_beta\_model(n\_primes=8, input\_dim=10):

model = models.Sequential(\[

layers.Dense(64, activation=\'relu\', input\_shape=(input\_dim,)),

### **5. Loss Function**

A composite loss to encourage physical consistency:

python

CopyEdit

def custom\_loss(y\_true, y\_pred):

alpha\_pred = tf.sigmoid(y\_pred\[:, :-1\])

beta\_pred = tf.tanh(y\_pred\[:, -1:\])

mse\_loss = tf.reduce\_mean((alpha\_pred - y\_true\[:, :-1\])\*\*2 +
(beta\_pred - y\_true\[:, -1:\])\*\*2)

entropy\_loss = -tf.reduce\_mean(alpha\_pred \* tf.math.log(alpha\_pred
+ 1e-8)) \# sparsity

return mse\_loss + 0.01 \* entropy\_loss

import numpy as np

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from scipy.linalg import norm, eigvals

\# Define simulation parameters

P\_N = \[2, 3, 5, 7\]

alpha = \[1/p for p in P\_N\] \# α\_p\_i = 1/p\_i for stability

beta = 0.5

M\_t = np.array(\[\[0.8, 0.1\], \[0.1, 0.6\]\]) \# Simple 2x2
interaction tensor

gamma = 0.1

epsilon = 0.05

steps = 30

def F\_t(t):

decay = epsilon \* np.exp(-gamma \* t)

return decay \* np.random.randn(2, 2)

\# Initialize state

Xi = np.eye(2)

Xi\_history = \[\]

eigenvalue\_history = \[\]

for t in range(steps):

meta\_op = sum(alpha\[i\] \* (p \*\* beta) \* M\_t for i, p in
enumerate(P\_N))

Xi = meta\_op @ Xi + F\_t(t)

Xi\_history.append(Xi.copy())

eigenvalue\_history.append(eigvals(Xi))

\# Convert eigenvalues to real parts for plotting

eig\_real = np.array(\[\[eig.real for eig in eigs\] for eigs in
eigenvalue\_history\])

\# Plot eigenvalue trajectories

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))

plt.plot(range(steps), eig\_real\[:, 0\], label=\'λ₁ (real)\')

plt.plot(range(steps), eig\_real\[:, 1\], label=\'λ₂ (real)\')

plt.title(\"Eigenvalue Trajectories of Ξ(t)\")

plt.xlabel(\"Time Step t\")

plt.ylabel(\"Eigenvalue (Real Part)\")

plt.legend()

plt.grid(True)

plt.tight\_layout()

plt.show()

import sympy

\# Generate Mersenne primes (p such that 2\^p - 1 is prime, with p
itself prime)

def mersenne\_primes(n):

count, p, mersennes = 0, 2, \[\]

while count \< n:

if sympy.isprime(p):

m = 2\*\*p - 1

if sympy.isprime(m):

mersennes.append(m)

count += 1

p += 1

return mersennes

\# Generate Gaussian primes in a region (limit real and imaginary parts)

def gaussian\_primes(limit):

primes = \[\]

for a in range(-limit, limit + 1):

for b in range(-limit, limit + 1):

if a == 0 and b == 0:

continue

z = a + b \* 1j

if sympy.isprime(int(abs(z.real)\*\*2 + abs(z.imag)\*\*2)):

primes.append(z)

return primes\[:10\] \# Limit to 10 for visualization

\# Display both sets

mersenne = mersenne\_primes(5)

gaussian = gaussian\_primes(10)

\\( \\beta = 0.5 \\) (sublinear). 3. Initialize \\( M\_t \\) as a random

python

import numpy as np

from scipy.linalg import norm, kron

\# === Mersenne Prime ELM ===

Mersenne\_primes = \[3, 7, 31, 127, 8191\]

alpha\_M = \[0.1 / np.log(p) for p in Mersenne\_primes\]

beta\_M = 0.5

Mt\_M = np.random.randn(4, 4) \# Replace with unitary matrix for quantum
cases

Mt\_M /= norm(Mt\_M, \'fro\') \# Normalize

def simulate\_Mersenne\_ELM(steps=100):

Xi = np.eye(4)

history = \[\]

for t in range(steps):

F\_t = 0.01 \* np.exp(-0.1 \* t) \* np.random.randn(4, 4)

meta\_op = sum(a \* (p \*\* beta\_M) \* Mt\_M for a, p in zip(alpha\_M,
Mersenne\_primes))

Xi = meta\_op @ Xi + F\_t

history.append(Xi)

return history

\# === Gaussian Prime ELM ===

Gaussian\_primes = \[-10-9j, -10-7j, -10-3j, -10-1j, -10+1j, -10+3j,
-10+7j, -10+9j, -9-10j, -9-4j\]

alpha\_G = \[0.05 / (p.real\*\*2 + p.imag\*\*2) for p in
Gaussian\_primes\]

beta\_G = 0.8 + 0.2j

sigma\_x = np.array(\[\[0, 1\], \[1, 0\]\])

sigma\_z = np.array(\[\[1, 0\], \[0, -1\]\])

Mt\_G = kron(sigma\_x, sigma\_z) \# Example entangling operator

def simulate\_Gaussian\_ELM(steps=100):

Xi = np.eye(4, dtype=complex)

history = \[\]

for t in range(steps):

F\_t = 0.02 \* np.exp(-0.05 \* t) \* (np.random.randn(4, 4) + 1j \*
np.random.randn(4, 4))

meta\_op = sum(a \* (abs(p) \*\* beta\_G) \* Mt\_G for a, p in
zip(alpha\_G, Gaussian\_primes)) \# abs(p) = norm

Xi = meta\_op @ Xi + F\_t

history.append(Xi)

return history

\-\-- \#\#\# \*\*4. Visualization & Analysis\*\* \#\#\#\# \*\*Key
Plots\*\* 1. \*\*Mersenne ELM\*\*: - Plot singular values of \\( \\Xi(t)
\\) over time (expect monotonic decay). - Phase diagram of \\(
\\alpha\_{M\_p} \\) vs. convergence rate. 2. \*\*Gaussian ELM\*\*: -
Heatmap of \\( \\text{Im}(\\lambda\_i(\\Xi(t))) \\) (captures rotational
dynamics). - Entanglement entropy \\( S(t) = -\\sum \\lambda\_i \\log
\\lambda\_i \\), where \\( \\lambda\_i \\) are eigenvalues of \\(
\\Xi(t) \\). \#\#\#\# \*\*Tools\*\*: - Use matplotlib for 2D plots and
plotly for interactive 3D state-space trajectories. \-\-- \#\#\# \*\*5.
Recommendations for Further Work\*\* - \*\*Quantum Hardware Test\*\*:
Deploy Gaussian ELM on IBM Quantum (use qiskit to compile \\( M\_t \\)).
- \*\*Topological Analysis\*\*: Compute Betti numbers of \\( \\Xi(t)

Application\*\*: Hash a message \\( m \\) as \\( H(m) =
\\text{argmin}\_p \\\| \\Xi\_{\\text{ELM}}(t\|m) - p \\\| \\) for \\( p
\\in P\_N \\). \-\-- \#\#\# \*\*Final Answer\*\* To proceed: 1. \*\*Run

import numpy as np

from scipy.integrate import dblquad

def sierpinski\_measure(x, y, D=1.585): \# D = log2(3)

return (x\*\*2 + y\*\*2)\*\*(D/2 - 1) \# Hausdorff measure

def fractal\_sobolev\_norm(u, s=1, D=1.585):

def integrand(x, y):

diff = u(x) - u(y)

return (abs(diff)\*\*2) / (abs(x - y)\*\*(D + 2\*s)) \*
sierpinski\_measure(x, y)

return dblquad(integrand, 0, 1, 0, 1)\[0\]

\#\#\#\# \*\*B. Fractal Laplacian\*\*

python

from scipy.special import gamma

def fractal\_laplacian(u, x, s=0.5, D=1.585):

C = gamma(D/2) / (2\*\*(2\*s) \* np.pi\*\*(D/2) \* gamma(1 - s))

def kernel(y):

return (u(x) - u(y)) / (np.linalg.norm(x - y)\*\*(D + 2\*s))

return C \* quad(kernel, -np.inf, np.inf, points=\[x\])\[0\]

\*\*Key Insight:\*\* - sierpinski\_measure weights integrals by
\*\*fractal dimension\*\*. - fractal\_laplacian captures \*\*anomalous
diffusion\*\* (e.g., quantum tunneling in prime gaps). \-\-- \#\#\#

import numpy as np

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from matplotlib import cm

\# Parameters

primes = \[2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13\]

T = 50 \# Number of time steps

grid\_size = len(primes)

alpha = {p: np.random.uniform(0.1, 0.9) for p in primes}

beta = 0.5

gamma = 0.1

\# Initialize Ξ\_dyn(t)

xi\_dyn = np.zeros((T, grid\_size))

F = lambda t: np.exp(-gamma \* t) \* np.random.normal(0, 0.05,
grid\_size)

\# Initial condition

xi\_dyn\[0\] = np.random.rand(grid\_size)

\# Mock interaction matrix M\[t\] as identity for simplicity

M = \[np.identity(grid\_size) for \_ in range(T)\]

\# Recursive update

for t in range(1, T):

xi\_sum = np.zeros(grid\_size)

for i, p in enumerate(primes):

xi\_sum += alpha\[p\] \* (p \*\* beta) \* M\[t\]\[i\] \*
xi\_dyn\[t-1\]\[i\]

xi\_dyn\[t\] = xi\_sum + F(t)

\# Generate heatmap

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 6))

cax = ax.imshow(xi\_dyn.T, aspect=\'auto\', cmap=cm.inferno,

extent=\[0, T, primes\[-1\], primes\[0\]\])

ax.set\_title(\"Recursive Prime-Indexed Entropy Evolution (Ξ\_dyn)\")

ax.set\_xlabel(\"Time Step\")

ax.set\_ylabel(\"Prime Index\")

fig.colorbar(cax, label=\"Ξ\_dyn Amplitude\")

plt.tight\_layout()

plt.show()

def stable\_selberg\_mqem(x, alpha, beta):

product = np.ones\_like(x)

epsilon = 1e-3 \# Increase epsilon for stability

term = (1 - alpha / (x\*\*2 + epsilon))

\# Log-transformed, normalized Selberg-MQEM model

def log\_selberg\_mqem(x, alpha, beta):

log\_sum = np.zeros\_like(x)

epsilon = 1e-3

for p in \[2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19\]:

term = 1 - alpha / (x\*\*2 + epsilon)

term = np.clip(term, 1e-5, None)

log\_sum += -beta \* np.log(term)

return np.exp(log\_sum) / np.sum(np.exp(log\_sum)) \# Normalize for
smooth fitting

\# Fit the log-space model

popt\_log, \_ = curve\_fit(log\_selberg\_mqem, bin\_centers, hist\_qg,
bounds=(\[0, 0\], \[1, 5\]))

\# Plot results

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 6))

plt.plot(bin\_centers, hist\_gr, \'o-\', label=\'GR Peak Distribution\',
alpha=0.6)

plt.plot(bin\_centers, hist\_qg, \'o-\', label=\'QG Peak Distribution\',
alpha=0.6)

plt.plot(bin\_centers, log\_selberg\_mqem(bin\_centers, \*popt\_log),
\'k\--\',

label=f\'Log Selberg-MQEM Fit (α={popt\_log\[0\]:.2f},
β={popt\_log\[1\]:.2f})\')

plt.title(\"Log-Space Normalized Selberg-MQEM Fit to Quantum Gravity
Peaks\")

plt.xlabel(\"Peak Amplitude\")

plt.ylabel(\"Normalized Density\")

plt.legend()

plt.grid(True)

plt.show()

Step 1: Define the System We have three bodies with the following
properties: • Masses: Each mass m\_1, m\_2, m\_3 = 1 \\, \\text{kg} •
Initial Positions: • \\vec{r}\_1 = (1, 0, 0) • \\vec{r}\_2 = (-1, 0, 0)
• \\vec{r}\_3 = (0, 1, 0) • Initial Velocities: • \\vec{v}\_1 = (0, 1,
0) • \\vec{v}\_2 = (0, -1, 0) • \\vec{v}\_3 = (-1, 0, 0) Step 2:
Implement the Equations of Motion The gravitational force between two
bodies is given by: \\vec{F}\_{ij} = G \\frac{m\_i m\_j}{\|\\vec{r}\_i -
\\vec{r}\_j\|\^3} (\\vec{r}\_j - \\vec{r}\_i) Where G is the
gravitational constant. Step 3: Numerical Integration To update the
positions and velocities, we will use the Euler method for simplicity:
1. Update Velocities: \\vec{v}\_i(t + \\Delta t) = \\vec{v}\_i(t) +
\\frac{\\vec{F}\_i}{m\_i} \\Delta t 2. Update Positions: \\vec{r}\_i(t +
\\Delta t) = \\vec{r}\_i(t) + \\vec{v}\_i(t) \\Delta t Step 4: Full
Simulation Code Here's a more organized and simplified version of the
code, ensuring clarity in the import numpy as np import
matplotlib.pyplot as plt \# Constants G = 6.67430e-11 \# Gravitational
constant in m\^3 kg\^-1 s\^-2 \# Masses of the bodies m1 = m2 = m3 = 1
\# Mass in kg \# Initial positions (x, y, z) r1 = np.array(\[1, 0, 0\])
r2 = np.array(\[-1, 0, 0\]) r3 = np.array(\[0, 1, 0\]) \# Initial
velocities (vx, vy, vz) v1 = np.array(\[0, 1, 0\]) v2 = np.array(\[0,
-1, 0\]) v3 = np.array(\[-1, 0, 0\]) \# Time settings dt = 0.01 \# Time
step in seconds total\_time = 10.0 \# Total simulation time in seconds
num\_steps = int(total\_time / dt) \# Number of steps \# Lists to store
positions for plotting positions1 = \[\] positions2 = \[\] positions3 =
\[\] \# Simulation loop for step in range(num\_steps): \# Calculate
distances r12 = r2 - r1 r13 = r3 - r1 r23 = r3 - r2 d12 =
np.linalg.norm(r12) d13 = np.linalg.norm(r13) d23 = np.linalg.norm(r23)
\# Calculate forces F12 = G \* m1 \* m2 / d12\*\*3 \* r12 F13 = G \* m1
\* m3 / d13\*\*3 \* r13 F23 = G \* m2 \* m3 / d23\*\*3 \* r23 \# Update
velocities v1 += (F12 + F13) / m1 \* dt v2 += (-F12 + F23) / m2 \* dt v3
+= (-F13 - F23) / m3 \* dt \# Update positions r1 += v1 \* dt r2 += v2
\* dt r3 += v3 \* dt \# Store positions for plotting
positions1.append(r1.copy()) positions2.append(r2.copy())
positions3.append(r3.copy()) \# Convert lists to numpy arrays for easy
indexing positions1 = np.array(positions1) positions2 =
np.array(positions2) positions3 = np.array(positions3) \# Plotting the
results plt.figure(figsize=(10, 10)) plt.plot(positions1\[:, 0\],
positions1\[:, 1\], label=\'Body 1\', color=\'blue\')
plt.plot(positions2\[:, 0\], positions2\[:, 1\], label=\'Body 2\',

quantum mechanical inner product formalism), we'll reframe each product
a⃗⋅b⃗\\vec{a} \\cdot \\vec{b}a⋅b as ⟨a∣O\^∣b⟩\\langle a \| \\hat{O} \| b

Here is your **revised Python code** using np.vdot (complex inner
product, which serves as the projection operator when O\^=I\\hat{O} =
IO\^=I) to simulate operator projections:

python

CopyEdit

import numpy as np

\# Constants

hbar = 1.054571817e-34 \# Reduced Planck\'s constant (J·s)

\# Example vectors (can be complex-valued for full generality)

E = np.array(\[1, 2, 3, 4\], dtype=complex) \# Energy eigenstate

phi = np.array(\[0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4\], dtype=complex) \# Wave function
state

B = np.array(\[0.5, 0.6\], dtype=complex)

tau = np.array(\[0.7, 0.8\], dtype=complex)

P = np.array(\[0.9, 1.0\], dtype=complex)

psi = np.array(\[1.1, 1.2\], dtype=complex)

\# Define projection-based QFE

def compute\_qfe\_projected(E, phi, B, tau, P, psi):

proj\_E\_phi = np.vdot(phi, E) \# \<phi\|E\>

proj\_B\_tau = np.vdot(tau, B) \# \<tau\|B\>

proj\_P\_psi = np.vdot(psi, P) \# \<psi\|P\>

return (hbar / 2) \* proj\_E\_phi \* proj\_B\_tau \* proj\_P\_psi

\# Compute

QFE\_projected = compute\_qfe\_projected(E, phi, B, tau, P, psi)

print(\"Quantum Field Equation (QFE) with operator projections:\",
QFE\_projected)

### **🧭 Theoretical Note:**

Using ⟨ϕ∣E⟩\\langle \\phi \| E \\rangle⟨ϕ∣E⟩, ⟨τ∣B⟩\\langle \\tau \| B
\\rangle⟨τ∣B⟩, etc., you are projecting one state onto
another---encoding how one observable (e.g., energy) is distributed over

### **🧠 Construction Outline: QFE Hash Function**

#### **1. Quantum Projection Inputs:**

Use inner products like:

Hi=⟨ϕi∣O\^i∣ψi⟩H\_i = \\langle \\phi\_i \| \\hat{O}\_i \| \\psi\_i
\\rangleHi​=⟨ϕi​∣O\^i​∣ψi​⟩

for states ϕi,ψi\\phi\_i, \\psi\_iϕi​,ψi​ and operators
O\^i\\hat{O}\_iO\^i​. For simplicity, start with O\^i=I\\hat{O}\_i =
IO\^i​=I.

#### **2. Encoding Strategy:**

Transform complex projection values into a binary representation via:

### **🧪 Python Implementation**

python

CopyEdit

import numpy as np

import hashlib

\# Example quantum states

phi = np.array(\[1+1j, 2-1j, 3+0.5j, 4-0.5j\])

psi = np.array(\[0.5+0.2j, 0.6-0.3j, 0.7+0.1j, 0.8+0.9j\])

tau = np.array(\[1.2+0.1j, 1.3-0.2j\])

B = np.array(\[0.9+0.3j, 1.1+0.4j\])

\# QFE-based projections

proj\_phi\_psi = np.vdot(phi, psi) \# \<phi\|psi\>

proj\_B\_tau = np.vdot(tau, B) \# \<tau\|B\>

\# Composite quantum field product

qfe\_value = proj\_phi\_psi \* proj\_B\_tau

\# Convert to fixed-length binary-friendly string

def encode\_complex(z):

re = np.real(z)

im = np.imag(z)

return f\"{re:.16f}{im:.16f}\"

\# Hash function using SHA-256

def qfe\_hash(z):

encoded = encode\_complex(z)

return hashlib.sha256(encoded.encode(\'utf-8\')).hexdigest()

\# Result

qfe\_digest = qfe\_hash(qfe\_value)

print(\"QFE-Derived Hash:\", qfe\_digest)

### **🧪 Python Code: QFE as Recurrent Activation**

CopyEdit

import numpy as np

\# Constants

hbar = 1.054571817e-34

\# Simulated quantum field vectors (complex domain)

phi\_t = np.random.rand(4) + 1j \* np.random.rand(4)

E\_t = np.random.rand(4) + 1j \* np.random.rand(4)

tau\_t = np.random.rand(2) + 1j \* np.random.rand(2)

B\_t = np.random.rand(2) + 1j \* np.random.rand(2)

psi\_t = np.random.rand(2) + 1j \* np.random.rand(2)

P\_t = np.random.rand(2) + 1j \* np.random.rand(2)

\# QFE activation

def qfe\_activation(phi, E, tau, B, psi, P):

return (hbar / 2) \* np.vdot(phi, E) \* np.vdot(tau, B) \* np.vdot(psi,
P)

\# Neural manifold evolution

def recursive\_step(h\_t, W, phi, E, tau, B, psi, P):

qfe = qfe\_activation(phi, E, tau, B, psi, P)

return np.tanh(W @ h\_t + qfe.real) \# Only real part activates

\# Init state and weight matrix

h\_0 = np.random.randn(3)

W = np.random.randn(3, 3)

\# Simulate manifold evolution

h\_t = h\_0

trajectory = \[h\_t.copy()\]

for \_ in range(50):

h\_t = recursive\_step(h\_t, W, phi\_t, E\_t, tau\_t, B\_t, psi\_t,
P\_t)

trajectory.append(h\_t.copy())

\# Visualization

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

trajectory = np.array(trajectory)

plt.plot(trajectory)

plt.title(\'Recursive Neural Manifold: QFE Activation Trajectory\')

plt.xlabel(\'Time Step\')

plt.ylabel(\'Activation Value\')

plt.legend(\[\'h₁\', \'h₂\', \'h₃\'\])

plt.grid(True)

plt.show()
